. This gate has an associated unitary matrix
U_{f}
, which is defined by its action on the computational basis as

U_{f}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{i}\right\rangle}={\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{i\oplus f(x)}\right\rangle},

where
x
is a
n
-bit string and
i
is a bit. The first register has
n
qubits and the second register has one qubit. If we take
i=0
, the above equation reduces to

U_{f}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{0}\right\rangle}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}l}{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}{\left|{1}\right\rangle},&\hbox{if $x=x_{0}$,}\\
{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{0}\right\rangle},&\hbox{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

which describes the output of a multiqubit Toffoli gate activated by
x_{0}
(and only by
x_{0}
) when the input is
{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{0}\right\rangle}
. The result of the calculation of
f(x)
is stored in the second register while the state of the first register remains unchanged.

For example, the circuit that implements
U_{f}
in the case
N=8
and
x_{0}=6
, that is,
f(110)=1
and
f(j)=0
if
j\neq 110
, is

\textstyle{{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{1}\right\rangle}.}

The first register has three qubits. Note that the state of the second register changes from
{\left|{0}\right\rangle}
to
{\left|{1}\right\rangle}
only if the input to the first register is
{\left|{110}\right\rangle}
because 110 activates the three controls and all other 3-bit strings do not.

In Grover’s algorithm, the state of the second register is always

{\left|{-}\right\rangle}=\frac{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}-{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}}.

Using linearity, the action of
U_{f}
is given by

U_{f}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{-}\right\rangle}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}l}-{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}{\left|{-}\right\rangle},&\hbox{if $x=x_{0}$,}\\
\,\,\,\,{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{-}\right\rangle},&\hbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

The same result is obtained from Proposition
3.1
on Page
3.1
, which states that

U_{f}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{-}\right\rangle}=(-1)^{f(x)}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{-}\right\rangle}.

9.3
The algorithm

Grover’s algorithm uses an additional operator defined as

G=\big(2\,{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{\textrm{d}}\right|}-I_{N}\big)\otimes I_{2},

where

{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}{\left|{j}\right\rangle}.

The notation “
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{\textrm{d}}\right|}
” denotes the
external product
between the vector
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
(a
N\times 1
matrix) and the dual vector
{\left\langle{\textrm{d}}\right|}
(a
1\times N
matrix). The external product is equivalent to the matrix product. Multiplying a
N\times 1
matrix by a
1\times N
matrix results in a
N\times N
matrix. Therefore,
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{\textrm{d}}\right|}
is a
N\times N
matrix, given by

{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{\textrm{d}}\right|}=\frac{1}{N}\begin{bmatrix}1&1&\cdots&1\\
1&1&\cdots&1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
1&1&\cdots&1\\
\end{bmatrix},

and

\big(2\,{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{\textrm{d}}\right|}-I_{N}\big)\,=\,\frac{1}{N}\begin{bmatrix}(2-N)&2&\cdots&2\\
2&(2-N)&\cdots&2\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
2&2&\cdots&(2-N)\\
\end{bmatrix}

which is called Grover matrix (or Grover operator).

Grover’s algorithm is described in Algorithm
9.1
.

Input:

An integer
N
and a function
f:\{0,...,N-1\}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
such that
f(x)=1
only for one point
x=x_{0}
in the domain.

Output:

x_{0}
with probability greater than or equal to
1-\frac{1}{N}
.

Prepare the initial state
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}{\left|{-}\right\rangle}
using
n+1
qubits;

1

Apply
{\left(G\,U_{f}\right)}^{t}
, where
t=\Big\lfloor\frac{\pi}{4}\sqrt{N}\Big\rfloor
;

2

Measure the first register in the computational basis.

Algorithm 9.1

Grover’s algorithm

9.4
Non-economical circuit of Grover’s algorithm

The goal of this Section is to find the circuit that implements the Grover operator using our understanding of implementing Boolean functions. The circuit initially uses more qubits than needed, but we will later demonstrate how to achieve a more resource-efficient version of the circuit.

To obtain the circuit, we have to do an algebraic manipulation with the expression of the Grover matrix

\big(2\,{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{\textrm{d}}\right|}-I_{N}\big)
. Note that

{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}=H^{\otimes n}{\left|{0}\right\rangle}

where
{\left|{0}\right\rangle}
is in the decimal notation and
H^{\otimes n}=H\otimes\dots\otimes H
. Transposing the above equation, we obtain

{\left\langle{\textrm{d}}\right|}={\left\langle{0}\right|}H^{\otimes n}.

Using
(H^{\otimes n})\cdot(H^{\otimes n})=(H\cdot H)^{\otimes n}=(I_{2})^{\otimes n}=I_{N}
, we obtain

\big(2\,{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{\textrm{d}}\right|}-I_{N}\big)=H^{\otimes n}\big(2\,{\left|{0}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{0}\right|}-I_{N}\big)H^{\otimes n},

where

\big(2\,{\left|{0}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{0}\right|}-I_{N}\big)=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&\cdots&0\\
\,\,\,\,0&-1&\cdots&0\\
\,\,\,\,\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\,\,\,\,0&0&\cdots&-1\\
\end{bmatrix}.

Matrix
\big(2\,{\left|{0}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{0}\right|}-I_{N}\big)
acts only on the first register. However, it is simpler to implement this matrix using both registers. Let us show that it is implemented by a multiqubit Toffoli gate activated by 0. Indeed, the action of
\big(2\,{\left|{0}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{0}\right|}-I_{N}\big)
on
{\left|{x}\right\rangle}
, where
x
is a
n
-bit string, is

\big(2\,{\left|{0}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{0}\right|}-I_{N}\big){\left|{x}\right\rangle}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}l}\,\,\,\,{\left|{0}\right\rangle},&\hbox{if $x=0$,}\\
-{\left|{x}\right\rangle},&\hbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Therefore, the action of
\big(2\,{\left|{0}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{0}\right|}-I_{N}\big)
on the first register is the same as the action of
(-U_{f^{\prime}})
on both registers when
x_{0}=0
(the state of the second register must be
{\left|{-}\right\rangle}
), where

f^{\prime}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}l}1,&\hbox{if $x=0$,}\\
0,&\hbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

The minus sign in
(-U_{f^{\prime}})
changes neither the result of the algorithm nor the final probability. That is, using
G
or
-G
in Grover’s algorithm does not change the final result.

Using these algebraic results, we conclude that a circuit that implements Grover’s algorithm is

repeat
\big\lfloor\frac{\pi}{4}\sqrt{N}\big\rfloor
times
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}_{1}}
\textstyle{H}

\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{i_{1}}
\textstyle{\vdots\hskip 8.5359pt}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\,\,\vdots}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}_{n}}
\textstyle{H}

\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{i_{n}}
\textstyle{{\left|{-}\right\rangle}}

\textstyle{U_{f}}
\textstyle{{\left|{-}\right\rangle},}

where bits
i_{1}
, …,
i_{n}
are the outputs of the measurements. Those bits are the bit of
x_{0}
, that is
x_{0}=(i_{1}\dots i_{n})_{2}
, with high probability.

9.5
Economical circuit of Grover’s algorithm

The second register of Grover’s algorithm can be discarded since it is possible to make a more economical implementation of the oracle
[
38
]
. Let us start by showing how to implement the operator
\big(2\,{\left|{0}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{0}\right|}-I_{N}\big)
(modulo a global phase), which enables us to implement operator
G
using only the first register. Let us show the equivalence of the following circuits:

\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{1}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{1}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{1}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{1}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{2}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{2}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{2}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{2}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\equiv}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{n-1}}\right\rangle}}\hskip 8.5359pt}
\textstyle{\hskip 8.5359pt{{\left|{k_{n-1}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{n-1}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{\left|{k_{n-1}}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{{\hskip 48.36958pt(-1)^{\bar{k}_{1}\cdots\bar{k}_{n}}{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}}.}
\textstyle{{{\left|{-}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{\hskip 42.67912pt{(-1)^{\bar{k}_{1}\cdots\bar{k}_{n}}{\left|{-}\right\rangle}}}

The output of the left-hand circuit is the
(n+1)
-qubit state

{\left|{k_{1}}\right\rangle}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}\otimes\left((-1)^{\bar{k}_{1}\cdots\bar{k}_{n}}{\left|{-}\right\rangle}\right),

which is obtained from the definition of the multiqubit Toffoli gate active only when qubits
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}
are set to 0. The Kronecker product has the property

{\left|{v_{1}}\right\rangle}\otimes(a{\left|{v_{2}}\right\rangle})=(a{\left|{v_{1}}\right\rangle})\otimes{\left|{v_{2}}\right\rangle}=a({\left|{v_{1}}\right\rangle}\otimes{\left|{v_{2}}\right\rangle}),

for any vectors
{\left|{v_{1}}\right\rangle}
,
{\left|{v_{2}}\right\rangle}
and any scalar
a
. Then, we move the scalar term
(-1)^{\bar{k}_{1}\cdots\bar{k}_{n}}
to the first register whose output is

{\left|{k_{1}}\right\rangle}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\left|{k_{n-1}}\right\rangle}\otimes\left((-1)^{\bar{k}_{1}\cdots\bar{k}_{n}}{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}\right)\,=\,(-1)^{\bar{k}_{1}\cdots\bar{k}_{n}}{\left|{k_{1}}\right\rangle}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}.

The state of the second register is
{\left|{-}\right\rangle}
and this register will be discarded at the end of the process.

Now let us show that the output of the right-hand circuit is the same. We use the fact that
XHXHX=-Z
, therefore the right-hand circuit is equivalent to

\textstyle{{\left|{k_{1}}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{k_{1}}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{k_{2}}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{k_{2}}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{{\left|{k_{n-1}}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{k_{n-1}}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{-Z}
\textstyle{(-1)^{\bar{k}_{1}\cdots\bar{k}_{n}}{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}.}

The output is obtained using that
(-Z){\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}=(-1)^{\bar{k}_{n}}{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}
, and since
-Z
is active only when qubits
k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}
are set to 0, we obtain the overall output
(-1)^{\bar{k}_{1}\cdots\bar{k}_{n-1}\bar{k}_{n}}{\left|{k_{1}\cdots k_{n-1}k_{n}}\right\rangle}
. In conclusion, the result of the first circuit (after the elimination of the second register) is the same as the result of the second circuit. Since we are going to perform a measurement of the first register only, this completes the proof that we can replace
without any loss
the first circuit with the second in Grover’s algorithm. This replacement is not valid in all algorithms. So far we have shown that
G
(modulo a global phase) can be implemented using only the first register.

Let us consider the oracle. If the oracle chooses
x_{0}=0
, the circuit of
U_{f}
is a multiqubit Toffoli gate active only when all qubits of the first register are set to 0. In this case, we have already shown how to implement
U_{f}
using only the first register. The oracle would use the right-hand circuit depicted in the beginning of this Section. If the oracle choose
x_{0}=1
, the circuit of
U_{f}
is a multiqubit Toffoli gate that is active only when all qubits of the first register are set to 0 except the
n
-th qubit, which is set to 1. In this case, we have the following circuit equivalence:

\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{1}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{1}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{1}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{1}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{2}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{2}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{2}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{2}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\equiv}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{n-1}}\right\rangle}}\hskip 8.5359pt}
\textstyle{\hskip 8.5359pt{{\left|{k_{n-1}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{n-1}}\right\rangle}}\hskip 5.69046pt}
\textstyle{\hskip 5.69046pt{{\left|{k_{n-1}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{{\hskip 48.36958pt(-1)^{\bar{k}_{1}\cdots\bar{k}_{n-1}k_{n}}{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}.}}
\textstyle{{{\left|{-}\right\rangle}}}
\textstyle{\hskip 62.59596pt{(-1)^{\bar{k}_{1}\cdots\bar{k}_{n-1}k_{n}}{\left|{-}\right\rangle}}}

The equivalence check is similar to the previous case but now it is obtained using that
HXH=Z
and
Z{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}=(-1)^{k_{n}}{\left|{k_{n}}\right\rangle}
, and since
Z
is active only when qubits
k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{n-1}
are set to 0, we obtain the overall output
(-1)^{\bar{k}_{1}\bar{k}_{2}\cdots\bar{k}_{n-1}k_{n}}{\left|{k_{1}k_{2}\cdots k_{n}}\right\rangle}
. This concludes the proof that the oracle would use a
n
-qubit circuit if
x_{0}=1
.

The remaining cases,
x_{0}\geq 2
, are obtained from the previous results. If the rightmost (last) bit of
x_{0}
is 0, we use the circuit equivalence described at the beginning of this Section in the following way: If any control (except the
n
-th) on the left-hand circuit changes from empty to full circle, the same must happen to the controls on the right-hand circuit. If the rightmost bit of
x_{0}
is 1, we use the second circuit equivalence of this Section, and in the same fashion if any control (except the
n
-th) on the left-hand circuit changes from empty to full circle, the same must happen to the controls on the right-hand circuit. The expression of the output changes accordingly.

Thus, not only
G
but also
U_{f}
can be implemented with
n
qubits by eliminating the second register and by introducing two Hadamard gates plus two Pauli
X
gates if the
n
-th qubit is activated by 0, and by introducing only two Hadamard gates if the
n
-th qubit is activated by 1.

Economical circuit when
N=4

The circuit of Grover’s algorithm in the economical form when
N=4
and
x_{0}=11
is

\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{1\,\,}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{1.}

The gates inside the first dashed box implement the oracle and the gates inside the second dashed box implement
\big(2\,{\left|{0}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{0}\right|}-I_{N}\big)
(modulo a global phase). This circuit can be simplified by substituting
HXH
in the second qubit with
Z
in two places.

The goal of Grover’s algorithm is to determine
x_{0}
by querying the oracle, that is, using the first dashed box, without looking at its implementation details. We have to pretend that the first dashed box is a black box. When
N=4
, there are four possible black boxes, case
x_{0}=11
is one of them. The gates that implement the oracle when
x_{0}=00
,
x_{0}=01
, and
x_{0}=10
are
(X\otimes XH)\text{CNOT}(X\otimes HX)
,
(X\otimes H)\text{CNOT}(X\otimes H)
, and
(I\otimes XH)\text{CNOT}(I\otimes HX)
, respectively. When
N=4
, the output is the correct one with probability 1.

Economical circuit for arbitrary
N

For an arbitrary
N
(
N=2^{n}
and
n\geq 2
), the circuit of Grover’s algorithm with only
n
qubits is

repeat
\big\lfloor\frac{\pi}{4}\sqrt{N}\big\rfloor
times
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}_{1}\,\,\,\,\,\,}
\textstyle{H}

\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{i_{1}}
\textstyle{\vdots\hskip 8.5359pt}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\,\,\vdots}

\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}_{n-1}}
\textstyle{H}

\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{i_{n-1}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}_{n}\,\,\,\,\,}
\textstyle{H}

\textstyle{u_{f}}
\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{{i_{n},}}

where the matrix
u_{f}
is the economical version of
U_{f}
and the circuit for
u_{f}
for an arbitrary
x_{0}=(i_{1}\dots i_{n})_{2}
is

\textstyle{X^{i_{1}}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{X^{i_{1}}}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\,\,\vdots}
\textstyle{\equiv}

\textstyle{X^{i_{n-1}}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{X^{i_{n-1}}}

\textstyle{u_{f}}
\textstyle{X^{1-i_{n}}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{X^{1-i_{n}}}
.

9.6
Analysis of Grover’s algorithm

Why does Grover’s algorithm work correctly? We answer this question using the economical form of the algorithm. The operators in this case are
u_{f}
, which is defined as

u_{f}\,=\,\sum_{x}(-1)^{f(x)}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{x}\right|},

and

g\,=\,2\,{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{\textrm{d}}\right|}-I_{N}.

Operator
u_{f}
is the economical version of
U_{f}
, that is,
u_{f}
is a
N
-dimensional operator whose action on the computational basis is

u_{f}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}l}-{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle},&\hbox{if $x=x_{0}$,}\\
\,\,\,\,{\left|{x}\right\rangle},&\hbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

In its turn,
g
is the economical version of operator
G
. The economical version of Grover’s algorithm is described in Algorithm
9.2
.

Input:

An integer
N
(power of 2) and a function
f:\{0,...,N-1\}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
such that
f(x)=1
only for one point
x=x_{0}
in the domain.

Output:

x_{0}
with probability greater than or equal to
1-\frac{1}{N}
.

Prepare the initial state
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
using
n
qubits ;

1

Apply
{\left(g\,u_{f}\right)}^{t}
, where
t=\Big\lfloor\frac{\pi}{4}\sqrt{N}\Big\rfloor
;

2

Measure all qubits in the computational basis.

Algorithm 9.2

Grover’s algorithm (economical version )

The goal of the algorithm is to find
x_{0}
, which is a
n
-bit string. It will be accomplished if the state of the qubits just before the measurement is
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
because the measurement in this case returns
x_{0}
. The analysis of the algorithm that we now start to describe is based on a geometric interpretation of vector reflections
[
1
]
. At the beginning of the algorithm, the state of the qubits is
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
. For large
N
,
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
is almost orthogonal to
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
. Fig.
9.1
shows vectors
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
and
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
, where
\theta/2
is the angle between
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
and the horizontal axis. Any other representation of those vectors can be used in the analysis of the algorithm provided that
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
is almost orthogonal to
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
.

Figure 9.1:
Depiction of vectors
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
and
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
.

The angle
\theta
is very small for large
N
, and in this case,
\theta/2
is a good approximation of
\sin(\theta/2)
.
In addition, the sine of an angle is equal to the cosine of the complement, that is,

\frac{\theta}{2}\approx\sin\frac{\theta}{2}=\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{\theta}{2}\right).

As
(\pi-\theta)/2
is the angle between
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
and
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
, by definition of the inner product,
\cos\,(\pi-\theta)/2
is the inner product of vectors
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
and
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
, the result of which is

\frac{\theta}{2}\approx\sin\frac{\theta}{2}=\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{\theta}{2}\right)=\left\langle x_{0}\big|\textrm{d}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}.

Therefore,

\theta\approx\frac{2}{\sqrt{N}}.

The first step of Algorithm
9.2
is the preparation of the initial state
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
. The next step is to apply
u_{f}
to
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
. The action of
u_{f}
on
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
(written in the computational basis) inverts the sign of the amplitude of
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
and does not change the other amplitudes. The amplitude of
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
is the orthogonal projection of
{\left|{\text{d}}\right\rangle}
on the vertical axis—see Fig.
9.1
, which is inverted by the action of
u_{f}
. Geometrically, the action of
u_{f}
is represented by a reflection of
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
about the horizontal axis. The angle between the vectors
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
and
(u_{f}{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle})
is
\theta
, as shown in Fig.
9.2
.

Figure 9.2:
Vector
u_{f}{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
is a reflexion of
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
about the horizontal axis.

The next step is to apply
g=\big(2\,{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{\textrm{d}}\right|}-I_{N}\big)
. Let us show that the action of
g
is a reflection about the axis defined by
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
. This proof is done in two parts. First, we show that
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
is invariant under the action of
g
. Second, we show that the action of
g
on
{\left|{\textrm{d}^{\perp}}\right\rangle}
inverts the sign of
{\left|{\textrm{d}^{\perp}}\right\rangle}
, where
{\left|{\textrm{d}^{\perp}}\right\rangle}
is any vector orthogonal to
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
. The first step follows from

g{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}=\big(2\,{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{\textrm{d}}\right|}-I_{N}\big){\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}=2\,{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}\left\langle\textrm{d}\big|\textrm{d}\right\rangle-{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}={\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle},

because
\left\langle\textrm{d}\big|\textrm{d}\right\rangle=1
. The second step follows from

g{\left|{\textrm{d}^{\perp}}\right\rangle}=\big(2\,{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{\textrm{d}}\right|}-I_{N}\big){\left|{\textrm{d}^{\perp}}\right\rangle}=2\,{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}\left\langle\textrm{d}\big|\textrm{d}^{\perp}\right\rangle-{\left|{\textrm{d}^{\perp}}\right\rangle}=-{\left|{\textrm{d}^{\perp}}\right\rangle},

because
\left\langle\textrm{d}\big|\textrm{d}^{\perp}\right\rangle=0
.

Figure 9.3:
Vector
g\,u_{f}{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
is a reflexion of
u_{f}{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
about
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
.

Fig.
9.3
depicts
g\,u_{f}{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
and shows that the action of
gu_{f}
rotates the initial state by
\theta
degrees towards
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
. Since
\theta
is a small angle, this achievement is modest but promising. It is easy to see that the second action of
gu_{f}
repeats the process of rotating
\theta
degrees towards
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
. We want to know how many iterations
r
are needed such as
r\theta=\pi/2
? The number of iterations is

r=\left\lfloor\frac{\pi}{2\theta}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor\frac{\pi}{4}\sqrt{N}\right\rfloor.

Figure 9.4:
Vector
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
is the final state before measurement and
a
is the norm of the projection of
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
on
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
. The angle between
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
and
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
is less than or equal to
\theta/2
.

It is still missing the calculation of the success probability. After
r
iterations, the state of the qubits is

{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}={\left(g\,u_{f}\right)}^{\big\lfloor\frac{\pi}{4}\sqrt{N}\big\rfloor}{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}.

Vector
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
is almost orthogonal to
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
at this point, as depicted in Fig.
9.4
. The angle between
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
and
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
is less than or equal to
\theta/2
.
The success probability is greater than or equal to the absolute square of the amplitude of
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
in the decomposition of
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
in the computational basis. This amplitude is
a
as shown in Fig.
9.4
. The orthogonal projection of
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
on the final state is at most
\cos(\theta/2)
. Therefore, the success probability
p=|a|^{2}
satisfies

\displaystyle p\,\,\geq\,\,\cos^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}\,\,\geq\,\,{1-\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}}\,\,\geq\,\,{1-\frac{1}{N}}.

The case
N=4
case is special because
\theta=60^{\circ}
, since
\sin(\theta/2)=1/\sqrt{N}
. With one application of
gu_{f}
, vector
{\left|{\textrm{d}}\right\rangle}
rotates
60^{\circ}
and coincides with
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
. In this case, the success probability is exactly
p=1
.

9.7
Solving SAT with Grover’s algorithm

The Boolean satisfiability problem (SAT) is the problem of determining if there exists an assignment of values that satisfies a given Boolean formula. For example, consider the Boolean formula

f(a,b,c)\,=\,a\wedge(c\vee(\bar{b}\wedge c)).

This formula is satisfiable because the assignment
abc=101
evaluates to True, that is,
f(1,0,1)=1
. Otherwise, the formula is unsatisfiable. In general, it is hard to decide whether the formula satisfiable or not because SAT is a NP-complete problem.

Since we can implement a circuit that evaluates the formula for any bit string as shown in Sec.
2.7
, we use this circuit as the oracle in Grover’s algorithm. If we know beforehand the number
m
of assignments of values that satisfies the formula, the number of iterations in the multimarked version of Grover’s algorithm
[
10
,
51
]
is

t=\left\lfloor\frac{\pi}{4}\sqrt{\frac{N}{m}}\right\rfloor.

To implement the circuit that evaluates formula
f(a,b,c)
, we use the techniques of Sec.
2.7
. Since the
f(a,b,c)
has exactly two assignments that evaluate to True,
abc=101
and
abc=111
, the number of iterations is
t=1
. Then, the circuit

\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{i_{1}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{i_{2}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{i_{3}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{-}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{\left|{-}\right\rangle}.}

returns a satisfying assignment
i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}
with probability 1, that is,
f(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})=1
. In general, the output has a success probability strictly smaller than 1, but greater than or equal to
1-1/N
. The only exception is the case
t=1
, which happens when
\theta=\pi/3
, or equivalently when

\sin\frac{\theta}{2}=\sqrt{\frac{m}{N}}=\frac{1}{2}.

The angle between vectors
{\left|{\text{d}}\right\rangle}
and
{\left|{x_{0}^{\perp}}\right\rangle}
is
\pi/6
and after one rotation, the state of the algorithm is
\pi/6+\pi/3=\pi/2
, exactly equal to the marked state
{\left|{x_{0}}\right\rangle}
, as can be seen from the analysis of Sec.
9.6
.

If we don’t know beforehand the number of assignments of values that satisfies the formula, we have to rerun Grover’s algorithm assuming that
m=1
, and then assuming
m=2
, and then
m=4
, and so on until at most
m=N/2
. After each attempt, we check whether the output is a satisfying assignment. This technique is described in
[
10
]
.

9.8
Final remarks

The same technique for implementing the oracle using
n
qubits analyzed in this Chapter can be applied to the implementation of the Deutsch-Jozsa algorithm with only
n
qubits. Note that the state of the second register in the Deutsch-Jozsa circuit before applying
U_{f}
is
{\left|{-}\right\rangle}
. What we have to do is to discard the second register and to replace
U_{f}
in the Deutsch-Jozsa circuit by
u_{f}
described at the end of Sec.
9.5
.

Chapter 10
Phase Estimation and Applications

Kitaev published the quantum phase estimation algorithm as a preprint in 1995
[
33
]
, and later as a section in a book in Russian, which was translated into English
[
34
]
. Kitaev’s method is based on a procedure for measuring an eigenvalue of a unitary
operator; that is, given a unitary operator
U
and one of its eigenvectors
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
, the algorithm finds the eigenvalue
\exp(2\pi\textrm{i}\phi)
, so that
U{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}=\exp(2\pi\textrm{i}\phi){\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
, where
\phi
is the phase of the eigenvalue. This algorithm provides an alternative way of factoring integers and calculating discrete logarithms. Not only that, it is used in many applications such as quantum counting.
This algorithm has been described in many books
[
4
,
6
,
28
,
31
,
47
,
65
]
.

10.1
Quantum phase estimation algorithm

Suppose we have a
{{n}}
-qubit unitary operator
U
and we know one of its eigenvectors
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
. We do not know the eigenvalue associated with
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
, but we know that its analytical expression is
\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi}
, where
0\leq\phi<1
(
\phi
is unknown), because
U
is unitary. We assume for now that
\phi=0.\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{m}
for some integer
{m}
, where
\phi_{1}
, …,
\phi_{m}
are bits, that is, the phase of the eigenvalue
\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi}
is a rational multiple of
2\pi
. The goal of the phase estimation algorithm is to determine
\phi
using
U
as an oracle and
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
as an input.

Basic block

The basic block of the circuit of the quantum phase estimation algorithm depends on an integer
0\leq j<m
and is given by

\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\frac{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}+\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi 2^{j}}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}}}
\textstyle{{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{/}^{{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U^{2^{j}}}
\textstyle{{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}.}

To verify the correctness of the output of the basic block, we have to use

U{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}\,=\,\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi}{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle},

and also

U^{2^{j}}{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}\,=\,\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi\,2^{j}}{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}.

The output of the basic block is obtained by applying the controlled
U^{2^{j}}
operator on
(H{\left|{0}\right\rangle})\otimes{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
, that is,

C\big(U^{2^{j}}\big)\left(\frac{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}+{\left|{1}\right\rangle}{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\,=\,\frac{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}+{\left|{1}\right\rangle}U^{2^{j}}{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}}\,=\,\frac{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}+\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi 2^{j}}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}}\otimes{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}.

Here we see an example of the
phase kickback process
because the phase was produced by the action of
U
on the second register but it appears as a relative phase of the first qubit after
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
has been collected.

There is an alternative way of writing the eigenvalue of
U^{2^{j}}
associated with
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
. Using that
\phi=0.\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{m}
in binary, then

\phi\,=\,\frac{\phi_{1}}{2}+\frac{\phi_{2}}{2^{2}}+\cdots+\frac{\phi_{m}}{2^{m}}.

Multiplying by
2^{j}
, we obtain

\phi\,2^{j}\,=\,2^{j-1}\phi_{1}+\cdots+2\phi_{j-1}+\phi_{j}+\frac{\phi_{j+1}}{2}+\cdots+\frac{\phi_{m}}{2^{{m}-j}}.

It is straightforward to check that

\exp\big(2\pi\textrm{i}\phi\,2^{j}\big)\,=\,\exp\left(2\pi\textrm{i}\left(\frac{\phi_{j+1}}{2}+\cdots+\frac{\phi_{m}}{2^{{m}-j}}\right)\right)

because
\exp\big(2\pi\textrm{i}\,2^{j-1}\phi_{1}\big)=\dots=\exp\big(2\pi\textrm{i}\phi_{j}\big)=1
. Then,

\exp\big(2\pi\textrm{i}\phi\,2^{j}\big)\,=\,\exp\left(2\pi\textrm{i}\,0.\phi_{j+1}\cdots\phi_{m}\right).

Note that the first digits of
\phi
were eliminated.

The implementation of
U^{2^{j}}
is not necessarily performed by
2^{j}
applications of
U
. This method is inefficient if
m
is large. The implementation depends on specific applications of the phase estimation algorithm. For instance, if
U
performs modular arithmetic, the
repeated squaring method
is employed.

The circuit of the quantum phase estimation algorithm has two blocks. The first is made of
m
basic blocks and the second is the inverse Fourier transform. Let us start by describing the first block.

First block

The circuit of first block comprises
m
basic blocks with a common second register as depicted in Fig.
10.1
. The first register has
{m}
qubits with input
{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}
and the second register has
{{n}}
qubits with input
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
. The output is a direct consequence of each basic block, which uses
U^{2^{j}}
, where
j
runs from 0 to
{m}-1
. The order of the controlled operations is irrelevant but
j
must be 0 for the
{m}
-th qubit,
j
must be 1 for the
({m}-1)
-th qubit, and so on.

\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\frac{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}+\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi 2^{{m}-1}}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\frac{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}+\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi 2^{{m}-2}}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}}}
\textstyle{\vdots\ \ }
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\frac{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}+\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi 2^{1}}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\frac{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}+\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi 2^{0}}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}}}
\textstyle{{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{/^{{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U^{2^{0}}}
\textstyle{U^{2^{1}}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{U^{2^{{m}-2}}}
\textstyle{U^{2^{{m}-1}}}
\textstyle{{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}}

Figure 10.1:
The first block of the phase estimation circuit is made of
m
basic blocks sharing the second register.

The output of the first register of the first block is

\frac{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}+\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi 2^{{m}-1}}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}}\otimes\frac{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}+\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi 2^{{m}-2}}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\frac{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}+\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi 2^{0}}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}}.

This output can be simplified into a very neat expression. In order to do so, let us replace each term with an equivalent term using a binary sum and collecting the denominators

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{\ell_{1}=0}^{1}\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi 2^{{m}-1}\ell_{1}}{\left|{\ell_{1}}\right\rangle}\otimes\sum_{\ell_{2}=0}^{1}\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi 2^{{m}-2}\ell_{2}}{\left|{\ell_{2}}\right\rangle}\otimes\cdots\otimes\sum_{\ell_{1}=0}^{m}\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi 2^{0}\ell_{m}}{\left|{\ell_{m}}\right\rangle}.

Pushing all sums to the beginning of the expression and combining all exponentials, we obtain

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{m}=0}^{1}\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi(2^{{m}-1}\ell_{1}+\cdots+2^{0}\ell_{m})}{\left|{\ell_{1}}\right\rangle}\otimes...\otimes{\left|{\ell_{m}}\right\rangle}.

Converting binary numbers into the decimal notation, we obtain

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\,\phi\,\ell}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}.

This is the neat expression we were looking for. Let us summarize the first block:

{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}\otimes{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}\xrightarrow[\text{ block }]{\text{first}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\,\phi\,\ell}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}\right)\otimes{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle},

where
m
is the number of qubits of the first register,
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
is an eigenvector of
U
with eigenvalue
\exp(2\pi\textrm{i}\,\phi)
, and
\phi=0.\phi_{1}...\phi_{m}
. In the next Subsection, we show that the output of the first register is

F_{2^{m}}{\left|{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}}\right\rangle},

where
F_{2^{m}}
is the Fourier transform, defined in Sec.
7.4
. Then, we write

{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}\otimes{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}\xrightarrow[\text{ block }]{\text{first}}\left(F_{2^{m}}{\left|{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}}\right\rangle}\right)\otimes{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle},

where
0.\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{m}
is the phase of the eigenvalue associated with eigenvector
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
of
U
.

Full circuit of the quantum phase estimation (QPE)

Now we show that the second block of the QPE algorithm is the inverse Fourier transform. In the last Subsection, we have shown that the output of the first register of the first block is

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\,\phi\,\ell}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}.

On the other hand, the action of the Fourier transform
F_{2^{m}}
on a generic state
{\left|{j}\right\rangle}
of the computation basis is

F_{2^{m}}{\left|{j}\right\rangle}\,=\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}j\ell}{2^{m}}}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}.

Inverting the equation, taking
j=\left(\phi_{1}...\phi_{m}\right)_{2}=(2^{m}\phi)_{10}
and
{\left|{j}\right\rangle}={\left|{\phi_{1}}\right\rangle}\otimes...\otimes{\left|{\phi_{m}}\right\rangle}
, we obtain

F_{2^{m}}^{\dagger}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{2\pi\textrm{i}\,\phi\,\ell}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}\right)\,=\,{\left|{2^{m}\phi}\right\rangle}\,=\,{\left|{\phi_{1}}\right\rangle}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\left|{\phi_{m}}\right\rangle}.

If we apply the inverse Fourier transform to the output of the first block, the result is a state of the computational basis equal to
{\left|{\phi_{1}}\right\rangle}\otimes...\otimes{\left|{\phi_{m}}\right\rangle}
. This means that a measurement in the computational basis reveals with certainty each fractional bit of
\phi
because we are assuming that
\phi
has been given with
m
bits. In the general case, the result of the algorithm is a good
m
-bit estimation of
\phi
, which is denoted by
\tilde{\phi}
, that is,
\tilde{\phi}\approx\phi 2^{m}
.

The full circuit of the phase estimation algorithm is depicted in Fig.
10.2
. The algorithm is described in Algorithm
10.1
.

\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\bullet}

\textstyle{F_{2^{m}}^{\dagger}}
\textstyle{\tilde{\phi}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\bullet}

\textstyle{\tilde{\phi}_{2}}
\textstyle{\vdots\ \ }
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\iddots}

\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\cdots}

\textstyle{\,\,\,\,\,\,\tilde{\phi}_{{m}-1}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\cdots}

\textstyle{\tilde{\phi}_{m}}
\textstyle{{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{/^{{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U^{2^{0}}}
\textstyle{U^{2^{1}}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{U^{2^{{m}-2}}}
\textstyle{U^{2^{{m}-1}}}
\textstyle{{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}}

Figure 10.2:
Full circuit of the QPE algorithm.

Input:

Eigenvector
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
of
U
.

Output:

Number
\tilde{\phi}
, where
\exp(2\pi\textrm{i}\phi)
is the eigenvalue of
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
and
\tilde{\phi}\approx\phi 2^{m}
.

Prepare the initial state
{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}\otimes{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
;

1

Apply
H^{\otimes{m}}
to the first register;

2

For
\ell
in
[0,{m}-1]
apply the controlled operation
C^{{m}-\ell}\left(U^{2^{\ell}}\right)
, where the control qubit is
{m}-\ell
and the target is the 2nd register;

3

Apply
F_{2^{m}}^{\dagger}
to the first register;

4

Measure the first register in the computational basis.

Algorithm 10.1

Quantum phase estimation algorithm

10.2
Application to order-finding

Let
N
and
a
be positive integers so that
1<a<N
and
\gcd(a,N)=1
. The multiplicative order of
a
modulo
N
is the smallest positive integer
r
that obeys

a^{r}\equiv 1\mod N.

Given
a
and
N
, the order-finding is the problem of calculating
r
. In this Section, we show how to solve the order-finding problem efficiently using the phase estimation algorithm providing an alternative to Shor’s factoring algorithm.

The strategy is to replace
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
in Algorithm
10.1
by
{\left|{1}\right\rangle}
(the second vector of the computational basis of the second register) and to choose
U
as the unitary operator that multiplies the input by
a
modulo
N
, that is,

U{\left|{y}\right\rangle}\,=\,{\left|{ay\mod N}\right\rangle}.

(10.1)

The input is a vector
{\left|{y}\right\rangle}
of the computational basis of the second register. We may think that
y
is represented in the decimal system. The output is also a vector
{\left|{y^{\prime}}\right\rangle}
of the computational basis of the second register, which is obtained by calculating
ay\equiv y^{\prime}
modulo
N
.
U
is a unitary operator because
\gcd(a,N)=1
.
U^{\dagger}
is defined accordingly using
a^{-1}
modulo
N
, that is,

U^{\dagger}{\left|{y}\right\rangle}\,=\,{\left|{a^{-1}y\mod N}\right\rangle}.

The motivation for using
U
here is that the repeated application of
U
produces successive powers of
a
, in fact,
U^{j}{\left|{y}\right\rangle}={\left|{a^{j}y}\right\rangle}
. The number of qubits
{{n}}
of the second register must be able to lodge
U
, then we take
{{n}}=\lceil\log_{2}N\rceil
.

In order to understand the order-finding as a phase estimation algorithm, let us find the eigenvectors of
U
. It is straightforward to obtain a 1-eigenvector because set
\{a^{0},a^{1},...a^{r-1}\}
, where
r
is the order of
a
modulo
N
, is invariant under the multiplication by
a
. Then, the normalized vector

{\left|{\psi_{0}}\right\rangle}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{r-1}{\left|{a^{\ell}}\right\rangle}

is a 1-eigenvector of
U
. The remaining ones are constructed using the entries of the Fourier transformation
F^{\dagger}_{r}
. Define

{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}\,=\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{r-1}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k\ell}{r}}{\left|{a^{\ell}}\right\rangle}.

(10.2)

Now let us check that each
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
is an eigenvector of
U
. In fact,

\displaystyle U{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{r-1}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k\ell}{r}}{\left|{a^{\ell+1}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{r-1}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k(\ell-1)}{r}}{\left|{a^{\ell}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k}{r}}{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}.

We conclude that
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
is an eigenvector of
U
with eigenvalue
\exp({2\pi\textrm{i}k}/{r})
for
0\leq k<r
, whose phase is
k/r
If we are able to prepare the input to the second register of the phase estimation algorithm as
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
for some
k
, we will obtain an approximation of
k/r
as the output, and then we find a candidate for the order of
a
using the continued fraction expansion. If the input to the second register is
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
, the output of the first block is

{\left|{0}\right\rangle}\otimes{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}\xrightarrow[\text{ block }]{\text{first}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k\ell}{r}}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}.

(10.3)

We cannot prepare
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
as an input to the phase estimation algorithm but we can find a known vector that is spanned by the set of vectors
\{{\left|{\psi_{0}}\right\rangle},...{\left|{\psi_{r}}\right\rangle}\}
. Using Eq. (
10.2
) and the geometric series, we show that

{\left|{a^{\ell}}\right\rangle}\,=\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k\ell}{r}}{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}.

The simplest choice is
{\left|{a^{0}}\right\rangle}={\left|{1}\right\rangle}
, which is given by

{\left|{1}\right\rangle}\,=\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}.

(10.4)

Using transformation (
10.3
) for each
k
, the output of the first block is

{\left|{0}\right\rangle}\otimes{\left|{1}\right\rangle}\xrightarrow[\text{ block }]{\text{first}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r2^{m}}}\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k\ell}{r}}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}.

To simplify the output, we use Eq. (
10.2
). Then,

output

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{r2^{m}}}\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k\ell}{r}}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{r-1}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k\ell^{\prime}}{r}}{\left|{a^{\ell^{\prime}}}\right\rangle}\right).

Inverting the order of the sums and combining the exponents, we obtain

output

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{r-1}\left(\frac{1}{r}\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k(\ell-\ell^{\prime})}{r}}\right){\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}{\left|{a^{\ell^{\prime}}}\right\rangle}.

Using the geometric series, the expression inside the parenthesis is 1 if
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
and 0 otherwise. This means that the output of the first block is

\displaystyle{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}\otimes{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{(n-1)}}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}\xrightarrow[\text{ block }]{\text{first}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}{\left|{a^{\ell}}\right\rangle}.

This is the same state as in the standard Shor’s factoring algorithm (state
{\left|{\psi_{2}}\right\rangle}
of Sec.
7.6
) just before applying the inverse Fourier transform
F_{2^{m}}
, that is, we consider the part of the algorithm where the input is
{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}{\left|{0}\right\rangle}
and then the Hadamard gate is applied on each qubit of the first register and then
U_{f}
, where
f(x)=a^{x}\mod N
:

\displaystyle{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}\otimes{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes n}\xrightarrow[\hskip 28.45274pt]{\,\,U_{f}\cdot(H^{\otimes m}\otimes I)\,\,}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}{\left|{a^{\ell}}\right\rangle}.

This means that the phase estimation version yields the same result and the analysis of the success probability is exactly the same as in Shor’s factoring algorithm if we choose
m
so that
m=\lceil 2\log_{2}N\rceil
. The number of qubits of the first register must be close to twice the number of qubits of the second register. Fig.
10.3
depicts the quantum part of Shor’s algorithm using the quantum phase estimation, where
U
is given by Eq. (
10.1
) and
a
is picked uniformly at random in
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{*}
. Note that the input
{\left|{1}\right\rangle}
to the second register is the second vector of the computational basis. This circuit replaces Algorithm
7.2
of Sec.
7.5
.

\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\bullet}

\textstyle{F_{2^{m}}^{\dagger}}
\textstyle{\ell_{0}}
\textstyle{\vdots\ \ }
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\iddots}

\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\cdots}

\textstyle{\,\,\,\,\,\,\ell_{{m}-2}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\cdots}

\textstyle{\,\,\,\,\,\,\ell_{m-1}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0...01}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{/^{{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U^{2^{0}}}
\textstyle{U^{2^{1}}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{U^{2^{{m}-1}}}

Figure 10.3:
Quantum part of Shor’s algorithm based on QPE.
U
is given by Eq. (
10.1
). The output is the same as the one described in Algorithm
7.2
of Sec.
7.5
.

There is an interesting special case. If we somehow know that the order
r
is a power of 2, we can take
m=n=\lceil\log_{2}N\rceil
. If
r
is a power of 2, the phase of the eigenvalue
\exp({2\pi\textrm{i}k}/{r})
is a rational multiple of
2\pi
, and the phase estimation algorithm returns an exact value
k2^{m}/r
. In this case, we do not need to calculate the continued fraction expansion of the result. Instead, we simply divide the output by
2^{m}
and select the denominator as the candidate for the order of
a
.

It is simpler to check that Shor’s factoring algorithm works correctly when we use Kitaev’s version. Let’s suppose that the order
r
is a power of 2. After compacting the circuit of Fig.
10.3
, the output of the QPE algorithm when the input is
{\left|{0}\right\rangle}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}
(in the decimal notation) is shown below

\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{/}^{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\,\,\text{QPE}_{U}\,\,}
\textstyle{\tilde{\phi}_{k}=\frac{k2^{m}}{r}}
\textstyle{{\left|{1}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{{/}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle},}

where
0\leq k<r
is selected uniformly at random. The result of the last circuit is obtained by using Eq. (
10.4
) and the fact that the phase of the eigenvalue associated with
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
is
\phi_{k}=k/r
. After applying the QPE algorithm, we obtain

{\left|{0}\right\rangle}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}\xrightarrow[\text{ }]{\,\,\,\,\text{QPE}_{U}\,\,\,\,}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{k=1}^{r-1}|{\tilde{\phi}_{k}}\rangle{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle},

where
\tilde{\phi}_{k}=\frac{k2^{m}}{r}
. Then, after a measurement of the first register, the output is necessarily
\tilde{\phi}_{k}
for some
0\leq k<1
picked uniformly at random, and the outcome of the second register is necessarily the eigenvector
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
. If the order
r
is not a power of 2, the output
\tilde{\phi}_{k}
is a good approximation for
\frac{k2^{m}}{r}
, and it is likely that
{\tilde{\phi}_{k}}
be a nearest integer to a multiple of
k/r
. A candidate
r^{\prime}
for the multiplicative order of
a
modulo
N
is obtained by selecting the convergent of the continued fraction expansion of
\tilde{\phi}_{k}/2^{m}
that has the largest denominator
r^{\prime}
such that
r^{\prime}<N
. The lower bound for the success probability of the quantum part determined in Sec.
7.6
is valid here.

How do we implement
U^{2^{j}}
efficiently for
0\leq j<m
? Ref.
[
40
]
addresses this question. Note that

U^{2^{j}}{\left|{y}\right\rangle}\,=\,{\left|{a^{2^{j}}y\mod N}\right\rangle}.

Since
a^{2^{j}}
can be calculated efficiently in
O(n^{2})
steps using the repeated squaring method, instead of applying
U
repeatedly
2^{j}
times, for each
j
we implement an operator
U_{j}{\left|{y}\right\rangle}={\left|{zy}\right\rangle}
after calculating
z=a^{2^{j}}
using the repeated squaring method. In this case, the first block can be computed in
O(n^{3})
steps.

10.3
Application to discrete logarithm

Let
N
,
a
, and
b
be known positive integers and let
s
be a positive integer such that
a^{s}\equiv b\mod N
and gcd
(a,N)=1
. Our goal is to find
s
given
N
,
a
, and
b
as input. This is the same problem addressed in Sec.
8.3
on Page
8.3
. Now we show how to solve the discrete logarithm problem using the phase estimation algorithm providing an alternative version to Shor’s algorithm for discrete logarithm.

The strategy we use here is the same one used in the order-finding algorithm based on the phase estimation. Recall that, when we described the order-finding algorithm, the output of the first block is the same as in the original Shor’s factoring algorithm right before the action of the inverse Fourier transform. Now, the state of the qubits right before the action of
F_{r}^{\dagger}\otimes F_{r}^{\dagger}
in Shor’s discrete logarithm algorithm described in Sec.
8.3
is

\frac{1}{r}\sum_{x,y=0}^{r-1}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{y}\right\rangle}{\left|{a^{x}b^{y}\mod N}\right\rangle}.

If we wish to produce this state using the phase estimation algorithm, we need to use three registers and two unitary operators:

\displaystyle U_{a}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\left|{ax\mod N}\right\rangle},

\displaystyle U_{b}{\left|{y}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\left|{by\mod N}\right\rangle}.

U_{b}
is a unitary operator because
\gcd(b,N)=1
. Indeed, the inverse of
b
is
a^{r-s}
, where
r
is the order of
a
modulo
N
. In the new algorithm, the action of
U_{a}
is controlled by the first register, and the action of
U_{b}
is controlled by the second register, as described in Fig.
10.4
. Note that
U_{a}
and
U_{b}
act on the same register.

\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}

\textstyle{F_{2^{m}}^{\dagger}}
\textstyle{\tilde{\phi}_{1}}
\textstyle{\vdots\ \ }
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\iddots}

\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}

\textstyle{\tilde{\phi}_{m}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}

\textstyle{F_{2^{m}}^{\dagger}}
\textstyle{\tilde{\phi}^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{\vdots\ \ }
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\iddots}

\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\bullet}

\textstyle{\tilde{\phi}^{\prime}_{m}}
\textstyle{{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{/^{{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U_{a}^{2^{0}}}
\textstyle{U_{a}^{2^{{m}-1}}}
\textstyle{U_{b}^{2^{0}}}
\textstyle{U_{b}^{2^{m-1}}}
\textstyle{{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}}

Figure 10.4:
Circuit of the discrete logarithm algorithm based on QPE, where
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
is a common eigenvector of
U_{a}
and
U_{b}
. Since we are not usually able to prepare
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
as the input to the third register, we use
{\left|{1}\right\rangle}
in its place, which can be represented as a linear combination of
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
, for
0\leq k<r
.

Let us check that the vectors

{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}\,=\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{r-1}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k\ell}{r}}{\left|{a^{\ell}}\right\rangle},

which are eigenvectors of
U_{a}
, are also eigenvectors of
U_{b}
. In fact, using that
b=a^{s}
, we have

\displaystyle U_{b}{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{r-1}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k\ell}{r}}{\left|{a^{\ell+s}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{r-1}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k(\ell-s)}{r}}{\left|{a^{\ell}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}ks}{r}}{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}.

We conclude that
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
is an eigenvector of
U_{b}
with eigenvalue
\exp({2\pi\textrm{i}ks}/{r})
for
0\leq k<r
. Better yet,
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
is an eigenvector of
U_{a}
and
U_{b}
simultaneously. If we are able to prepare the input to the third register of the circuit depicted in Fig.
10.4
as
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
for some
k
, we will obtain an estimate of
\tilde{\phi}\approx k/r
as the output of the first register and an estimate of
\tilde{\phi}^{\prime}\approx ks/r
as the output of the second register modulo
N
. If the input to the third register is
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
, the output of the first block is

{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}\xrightarrow[\text{ block }]{\text{first}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k\ell}{r}}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{m}}}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}ks\ell^{\prime}}{r}}{\left|{\ell^{\prime}}\right\rangle}\right){\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle},

where
m
is the number of qubits of the first and second registers, and
{{n}}=\lceil\log_{2}N\rceil
of the third register. The first term inside the parentheses is obtained by replacing
\phi
with
k/r
and the second term by replacing
\phi
with
ks/r
in the output of the first block of the phase estimation algorithm. Simplifying the output, we have

{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}\xrightarrow[\text{ block }]{\text{first}}\left(\frac{1}{2^{m}}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k(\ell+s\ell^{\prime})}{r}}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}{\left|{\ell^{\prime}}\right\rangle}\right){\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}.

Usually, we are not able to prepare
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
as an input to the phase estimation algorithm but we can use

{\left|{1}\right\rangle}\,=\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}

again instead of
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
.
In this case, the input to and output of the first block are

{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}\xrightarrow[\text{ block }]{\text{first}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\left(\frac{1}{2^{m}}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k(\ell+s\ell^{\prime})}{r}}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}{\left|{\ell^{\prime}}\right\rangle}\right){\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle},

where the input
{\left|{1}\right\rangle}
is written in the decimal notation. We use the definition of
{\left|{\psi_{k}}\right\rangle}
given by Eq. (
10.2
) to simplify the output. We start writing

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\left(\frac{1}{2^{m}}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k(\ell+s\ell^{\prime})}{r}}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}{\left|{\ell^{\prime}}\right\rangle}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{{k^{\prime}}=0}^{r-1}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k{k^{\prime}}}{r}}{\left|{a^{{k^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle}\right),

and by pushing the sums over
\ell
,
\ell^{\prime}
,
{k^{\prime}}
to the left and combining the exponents, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{m}}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=0}^{2^{m}-1}\sum_{{k^{\prime}}=0}^{r-1}\left(\frac{1}{r}\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\textrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\textrm{i}k(\ell+s\ell^{\prime}-{k^{\prime}})}{r}}\right){\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}{\left|{\ell^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\left|{a^{{k^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle}.

The expression inside the parenthesis is 1 if
\ell+s\ell^{\prime}={k^{\prime}}
and 0 otherwise. This means that the action of the first block is

\displaystyle{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}\xrightarrow[\text{ block }]{\text{first}}\frac{1}{2^{m}}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=0}^{2^{m}-1}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}{\left|{\ell^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\left|{a^{\ell+s\ell^{\prime}}}\right\rangle}.

Using that
a^{s}=b\mod N
,
\ell\rightarrow x
, and
\ell^{\prime}\rightarrow y
, we have

\displaystyle{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}{\left|{1}\right\rangle}\xrightarrow[\text{ block }]{\text{first}}\frac{1}{2^{m}}\sum_{x,y=0}^{2^{m}-1}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{y}\right\rangle}{\left|{a^{x}b^{y}}\right\rangle}.

The output of the first block is the same state as in the standard Shor’s discrete-logarithm algorithm just before applying the inverse Fourier transforms
F_{2^{m}}\otimes F_{2^{m}}
(state
{\left|{\psi_{2}}\right\rangle}
of Algorithm
8.1
on Page
8.1
). This means that the phase estimation version yields the same result and the analysis of the success probability is exactly the same as in Shor’s algorithm if we choose
m
appropriately.

10.4
Application to quantum counting

In the context of Grover’s algorithm, we have an oracle
f:\{0,...,N-1\}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
that is a Boolean function defined as

f(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}l}1,&\hbox{if $x\in M$,}\\
0,&\hbox{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

where
M
is a subset of the domain and
N=2^{n}
. We say that
x
is marked if
x\in M
. The optimal number of steps of Grover’s algorithm depends on
|M|
; indeed, it is given by
\frac{\pi}{4}\sqrt{N/|M|}
. If the cardinality of
M
is unknown, it is possible to find a marked element by repeatedly guessing the runtime of Grover’s algorithm
[
10
]
. An alternative method is by solving the quantum counting problem
[
11
]
.

The quantum counting problem asks what is the cardinality of
M
given function
f
as an oracle. A classical solution cannot perform better than
\Omega(N)
queries to the oracle because all domain elements must be checked. The quantum algorithm can find the solution in
O\big(\sqrt{|M|\,N}\big)
queries to the oracle.

Before addressing the quantum counting problem, let us review some key points of Grover’s algorithm with many marked elements, which is an extension of the algorithm presented in Chapter
9
. There is an economical version of the algorithm, which uses only one
n
-qubit register. The initial state is the uniform superposition of the computational basis given by

{\left|{\text{d}}\right\rangle}\,=\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}{\left|{j}\right\rangle},

and the algorithm consists of
\frac{\pi}{4}\sqrt{N/|M|}
applications of the evolution operator

U\,=\,G\,U_{f},

where

G\,=\,2{\left|{\text{d}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{\text{d}}\right|}-I

and

U_{f}\,=\,\sum_{x=0}^{N-1}(-1)^{f(x)}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{x}\right|}.

The analysis of the algorithm is performed by using the
\textrm{e}^{\pm{\textrm{i}\theta}}
-eigenvectors of
U
, which are given in terms of the superposition of marked states
1
1

1

Some references call
{\left|{M}\right\rangle}
as “good state” and
{\left|{M^{\perp}}\right\rangle}
as “bad state”.

{\left|{M}\right\rangle}
and the superposition of unmarked states
{\left|{M^{\perp}}\right\rangle}
:

{\left|{\psi^{\pm}}\right\rangle}\,=\,\frac{{\left|{M}\right\rangle}\pm\,\textrm{i}\,{\left|{M^{\perp}}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{2}},

(10.5)

where

\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\,=\,\sqrt{\frac{|M|}{N}},

and

\displaystyle{\left|{M}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|M|}}\sum_{x\in M}{\left|{x}\right\rangle},

\displaystyle{\left|{M^{\perp}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-|M|}}\sum_{x\not\in M}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}.

It is straightforward to check that
\left\langle M^{\perp}\big|M\right\rangle=0
, and

{\left|{\text{d}}\right\rangle}\,=\,\sqrt{\frac{|M|}{N}}\,{\left|{M}\right\rangle}+\sqrt{1-\frac{|M|}{N}}\,\,{\left|{M^{\perp}}\right\rangle}.

Using the equation above, the definition of
\sin(\theta/2)
, and Eq. (
10.5
), we obtain

{\left|{\text{d}}\right\rangle}\,=\,\frac{\textrm{e}^{\frac{\textrm{i}\theta}{2}}{\left|{\psi^{+}}\right\rangle}-\textrm{e}^{-\frac{\textrm{i}\theta}{2}}{\left|{\psi^{-}}\right\rangle}}{\textrm{i}\sqrt{2}}.

Now we come back to the quantum counting problem using the phase estimation algorithm. Since the eigenvalue of
{\left|{\psi^{+}}\right\rangle}
is
\exp(\textrm{i}\theta)
, where
\sin(\theta/2)=\sqrt{|M|/N}
, we would obtain an approximation for
|M|
if we use
{\left|{\psi^{+}}\right\rangle}
as the input to the second register of the phase estimation algorithm with
U=GU_{f}
. If we do not know how to prepare
{\left|{\psi^{+}}\right\rangle}
, then the strategy is to replace
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
in Algorithm
10.1
by a known vector that belongs to the subspace spanned by
{\left|{\psi^{+}}\right\rangle}
and
{\left|{\psi^{-}}\right\rangle}
. The best candidate is
{\left|{\text{d}}\right\rangle}
, which can be easily prepared by applying
H^{\otimes n}
to
{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes n}
. In this case, the number of qubits of the second register must be
{{n}}=\log_{2}N
and the output of the first block is

{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}{\left|{\text{d}}\right\rangle}\xrightarrow[\text{ block }]{\text{first}}\frac{\textrm{e}^{\frac{\textrm{i}\theta}{2}}}{\textrm{i}\sqrt{2^{m+1}}}{\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi^{+}\ell}}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}{\left|{\psi^{+}}\right\rangle}-\frac{\textrm{e}^{-\frac{\textrm{i}\theta}{2}}}{\textrm{i}\sqrt{2^{m+1}}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2^{m}-1}\textrm{e}^{{2\pi\textrm{i}\phi^{-}\ell}}{\left|{\ell}\right\rangle}{\left|{\psi^{-}}\right\rangle}},

where
\phi^{+}=\theta/2\pi
for the first term and
\phi^{-}=(2\pi-\theta)/2\pi
for the second term. After applying the inverse Fourier transform
F_{2^{m}}^{\dagger}
, we obtain the following output of the full circuit

\frac{\textrm{e}^{\frac{\textrm{i}\theta}{2}}}{\textrm{i}\sqrt{2}}\,{\left|{2^{m}\tilde{\phi}^{+}}\right\rangle}-\frac{\textrm{e}^{-\frac{\textrm{i}\theta}{2}}}{\textrm{i}\sqrt{2}}\,{\left|{2^{m}\tilde{\phi}^{-}}\right\rangle},

where
\tilde{\phi}
is a
m
bit estimate of
\phi
. After a measurement in the computational basis, we learn an estimate of
\phi^{+}
or
\phi^{-}
with equal chance. Let
\tilde{\phi}
be the measurement result. Using that that
\sin(\theta/2)=\sqrt{|M|/N}
,
\phi^{+}=\theta/2\pi
, and
\phi^{-}=(2\pi-\theta)/2\pi
, the estimate of
|M|
is
N\sin^{2}(\pi\tilde{\phi})
because for the first case we obtain an estimate of
|M|
using
|\widetilde{M}|=N\sin^{2}(\pi\tilde{\phi}^{+})
and for the second case
|\widetilde{M}|=N\sin^{2}(\pi-\pi\tilde{\phi}^{-})=N\sin^{2}(\pi\tilde{\phi}^{-})
.

How many qubits has the first register? This is the tricky part. Note that
m
cannot be equal to
n
because the number of applications of
U
would be
2^{0}+\cdots+2^{n-1}=2^{n}-1
. Then, the number of queries to
f
would be
O(N)
. If we choose
m=n/2
, the number of queries to
f
would be
\sqrt{N}
, but in this case we obtain an estimate
|\widetilde{M}|
so that

\big|\,|\widetilde{M}|-|M|\,\big|\,=\,O\big(\sqrt{|M|}\big).

(10.6)

This estimate is not good. For instance, suppose that
|M|
is around
N/2
. If we wish to know the number of marked elements, and we obtain
|\widetilde{M}|
with an error as big as
O(\sqrt{N})
, we don’t have a good result. To understand what is the problem here, which does not arise in the factoring and discrete logarithm algorithms, we have to analyze carefully the range of values of angle
\theta
we are trying to estimate.

For this analysis, let us assume that
0<|M|\ll N
, or more formally,
|M|=o(\sqrt{N})
. The expression
\sin(\theta/2)=\sqrt{|M|/N}
can be written asymptotically as

\theta=\frac{2\sqrt{|M|}}{\sqrt{N}}+O\left(\frac{|M|}{N}\right).

This means that
\theta/2\pi
represented in terms of binary digits is of the form
0.0\cdots 01\cdots
, where the number of 0’s before the first 1 is around
n/2-\log_{2}(|M|)/2
. If we choose the size of the first register so that
m
is less than
n/2-\log_{2}(|M|)/2
, it is likely that we obtain a 0 as the output of the counting algorithm, which is wrong. If we choose
m=n/2
, we will obtain around
\log_{2}(|M|)/2
correct significant bits of
\theta/2\pi
. This is an imprecise estimate of
|M|
compatible with Eq. (
10.6
). In fact,
|M|
has
\log_{2}(|M|)
bits, and we need to know most of the significant bits in order to have a good estimate of
|M|
.

Chapter 11
Final Remarks

Most quantum algorithms analyzed in this work can be cast into the oracle-based framework. The query complexity of an algorithm based on an oracle or a black-box is the number of queries. It does not matter how difficult it is to implement the oracle unless we aim to solve a practical problem. In practical problems, it is our task to implement the oracle, and then the cost of each evaluation matters. Take Shor’s factoring algorithm as an example. The oracle in this case is a
r
-periodic function and our goal is to find
r
. We have seen that the function in Shor’s algorithm is the modular exponentiation, which can be implemented efficiently in terms of the input size using the repeated squaring method.

Any classical deterministic algorithm can be represented as an
n
-input and
m
-output function
f:{0,1}^{n}\longrightarrow{0,1}^{m}
, which is a collection of
m

n
-bit Boolean functions. Therefore, any classical algorithm can be implemented on a quantum computer with two registers of sizes
n
and
m
using the operator

U_{f}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{y}\right\rangle}\,=\,{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{y\oplus f(x)}\right\rangle}.

To exploit quantum parallelism, we need to apply
H^{\otimes n}
to the first register before applying
U_{f}
. After applying
U_{f}
, we have a superposition state, which is not useful unless we perform some quantum post-processing that would produce the desired output. Most of the quantum algorithms we have analyzed can be cast into the following circuit:

\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}

\textstyle{\,\,\,U_{f}\,\,\,}

\textstyle{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{post}\\
\text{processing}\end{array}}
\textstyle{i_{0}}
\textstyle{\vdots\,\,\,}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}

\textstyle{i_{n-1}}

\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}}
\textstyle{{/^{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{j_{0}...j_{m-1}.}

For the Deutsch-Jozsa, Bernstein-Vazirani, Simon, and Shor (factoring) algorithms, the quantum post-processing is either applying Hadamard gates to all qubits or using the inverse Fourier transform. They have the structure outlined above with a few adaptations. Some of these algorithms also require classical post-processing, which is not represented in the quantum circuit.

Grover’s algorithm does not have the structure outlined above because the oracle and the post-processing are repeated many times before measurement. On the other hand, Grover’s algorithm has a polynomial gain in contrast with the exponential gain of Simon’s and Shor’s algorithms. The extension of the general structure that includes Grover’s algorithm is

repeat
k
times
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}

\textstyle{\,\,\,U_{f}\,\,\,}

\textstyle{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{post}\\
\text{processing}\end{array}}
\textstyle{i_{0}}
\textstyle{\vdots\,\,\,}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}

\textstyle{i_{n-1}}

\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{m}}}
\textstyle{{/^{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{j_{0}...j_{m-1}.}

The number of repetitions
k
is 1 for the Deutsch-Jozsa, Bernstein-Vazirani, Simon, and Shor; and
k
is
\lfloor\pi\sqrt{2^{n}}/4\rfloor
for Grover’s algorithm.
The measurement of the second register is unnecessary. It is there because it helps in the analysis of the algorithm.

The second register of the Deutsch-Jozsa, Bernstein-Vazirani, and Grover algorithms has only one qubit (
m=1
), whose state during the computation is
{\left|{-}\right\rangle}
, which is obtained by applying
X
and
H
on the last qubit before
U_{f}
. The oracle for those cases obeys

U_{f}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{-}\right\rangle}\,=\,(-1)^{f(x)}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}{\left|{-}\right\rangle}.

This means that the second register can be eliminated and there is an economical version of the circuit with the following form:

repeat
k
times
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}

\textstyle{\,\,\,u_{f}\,\,\,}

\textstyle{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{post}\\
\text{processing}\end{array}}
\textstyle{i_{0}}
\textstyle{\vdots\,\,\,}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}}
\textstyle{H}

\textstyle{i_{n-1}.}

As before,
k=1
for the Deutsch-Jozsa and Bernstein-Vazirani algorithms,
k=\lfloor\pi\sqrt{2^{n}}/4\rfloor
for Grover’s algorithm, and

u_{f}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}=(-1)^{f(x)}{\left|{x}\right\rangle}.

Shor’s algorithm for discrete logarithms shows us how to extend the structure of the circuit when the function
f
has more than one variable. Suppose that
f
has two variables. Then,
U_{f}
is defined as

U_{f}{\left|{x_{1}}\right\rangle}{\left|{x_{2}}\right\rangle}{\left|{y}\right\rangle}\,=\,{\left|{x_{1}}\right\rangle}{\left|{x_{2}}\right\rangle}{\left|{y\oplus f(x_{1},x_{2})}\right\rangle}.

This means that we need a circuit with three registers and the general structure of the algorithm is the same up to small changes, as follows:

repeat
k
times
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes n_{1}}}
\textstyle{{/^{n_{1}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{\otimes n_{1}}}

\textstyle{\,\,\,U_{f}\,\,\,}

\textstyle{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{post}\\
\text{processing}\end{array}}
\textstyle{i_{0}...i_{n_{1}-1}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes n_{2}}}
\textstyle{{/^{n_{2}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{\otimes n_{2}}}

\textstyle{i^{\prime}_{0}...i^{\prime}_{n_{2}-1}}
\textstyle{{\left|{0}\right\rangle}^{\otimes{n_{3}}}}
\textstyle{{/^{n_{3}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{j_{0}...j_{n_{3}-1}.}

Those recipes are useful to understand advanced algorithms.

,

, and
